People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I’m here because I haven’t been feeling like myself lately. The district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to my plate, on top of my regular teaching, and I can still get through the school day, I mean, I’m not falling apart in front of the kids or anything… but I feel so wound up and discouraged.

It’s odd because I used to be pretty on top of lesson plans and emails—maybe too on top of them, honestly—and now I find myself putting them off. I’ll sit down to do them and end up rearranging papers or thinking about something one of my students said, or, you know, how the district always launches these things like we all have extra hours hidden somewhere. So… that’s why I came in. It just doesn’t feel like me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.

Now, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.

I still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.

I came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.

And then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.

I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. So… that’s why I came in. It just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that broadly. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been really wound up and discouraged.\n\nI still get through the school day, and the kids wouldn’t necessarily know anything is off—I mean, they have enough going on, and honestly some of them are very perceptive—but when I sit down to do lesson plans or answer emails, things I used to just handle, I keep putting them off. So yes, anxious or tense around all of that, but it feels tied to this extra workload more than just all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, kind of discouraged, I guess. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of everything else, I’ve felt really wound up and just… not like myself.\n\nI still get through the school day—because you do, you know, the kids are there and they need you—but then the lesson plans and emails, things I used to handle without thinking, I keep putting off. And then I feel worse about that. So yes, sad in that sense, but it feels very tied to all this extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically liked to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really stopped to measure it that way. I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. It’s still happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really in the same way. If I’m truly not thinking about school, I can be okay—like if I’m watching some silly cooking show or talking to my sister about her garden, which is apparently a whole drama this year because the squirrels have become organized.

But the problem is school sort of sneaks in. Ever since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of everything else, I feel wound up and discouraged more than I usually would. I’m still getting through the day, I’m not falling apart, but I’ll realize I’m putting off lesson plans or emails that I used to just knock out without thinking. So it’s mostly tied to that, I think.

I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. It’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about school or work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. If I’m truly not thinking about school, I can be okay—like if I’m watching some silly cooking show or talking to my sister about her garden, which is apparently a whole drama this year because the squirrels have become organized.\n\nBut the problem is school sort of sneaks in. Ever since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of everything else, I feel wound up and discouraged more than I usually would. I’m still getting through the day, I’m not falling apart, but I’ll realize I’m putting off lesson plans or emails that I used to just knock out without thinking. So it’s mostly tied to that, I think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I haven’t been feeling like myself lately. The district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to my plate, on top of my regular teaching, and I can still get through the school day, I mean, I’m not falling apart in front of the kids or anything… but I feel so wound up and discouraged.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be pretty on top of lesson plans and emails—maybe too on top of them, honestly—and now I find myself putting them off. I’ll sit down to do them and end up rearranging papers or thinking about something one of my students said, or, you know, how the district always launches these things like we all have extra hours hidden somewhere. So… that’s why I came in. It just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that broadly. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been really wound up and discouraged.\n\nI still get through the school day, and the kids wouldn’t necessarily know anything is off—I mean, they have enough going on, and honestly some of them are very perceptive—but when I sit down to do lesson plans or answer emails, things I used to just handle, I keep putting them off. So yes, anxious or tense around all of that, but it feels tied to this extra workload more than just all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, kind of discouraged, I guess. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of everything else, I’ve felt really wound up and just… not like myself.\n\nI still get through the school day—because you do, you know, the kids are there and they need you—but then the lesson plans and emails, things I used to handle without thinking, I keep putting off. And then I feel worse about that. So yes, sad in that sense, but it feels very tied to all this extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically liked to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really stopped to measure it that way. I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. So it’s mostly tied to that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change, no. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I haven’t been feeling like myself lately. The district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to my plate, on top of my regular teaching, and I can still get through the school day, I mean, I’m not falling apart in front of the kids or anything… but I feel so wound up and discouraged.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be pretty on top of lesson plans and emails—maybe too on top of them, honestly—and now I find myself putting them off. I’ll sit down to do them and end up rearranging papers or thinking about something one of my students said, or, you know, how the district always launches these things like we all have extra hours hidden somewhere. So… that’s why I came in. It just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that broadly. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been really wound up and discouraged.\n\nI still get through the school day, and the kids wouldn’t necessarily know anything is off—I mean, they have enough going on, and honestly some of them are very perceptive—but when I sit down to do lesson plans or answer emails, things I used to just handle, I keep putting them off. So yes, anxious or tense around all of that, but it feels tied to this extra workload more than just all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, kind of discouraged, I guess. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of everything else, I’ve felt really wound up and just… not like myself.\n\nI still get through the school day—because you do, you know, the kids are there and they need you—but then the lesson plans and emails, things I used to handle without thinking, I keep putting off. And then I feel worse about that. So yes, sad in that sense, but it feels very tied to all this extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically liked to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really stopped to measure it that way. I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of failing. It’s more like… ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout onto my regular teaching load, I look at the lesson plans or the emails and I just feel wound up and discouraged before I even start.

I still get through the day, you know, I’m not collapsing in the copy room or anything—though that copier does test a person’s will to live—but I’ve noticed I’m putting off things I used to just handle without thinking. So maybe there’s some worry in there, but it feels more like being overloaded and not quite myself.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I haven’t been feeling like myself lately. The district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to my plate, on top of my regular teaching, and I can still get through the school day, I mean, I’m not falling apart in front of the kids or anything… but I feel so wound up and discouraged.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be pretty on top of lesson plans and emails—maybe too on top of them, honestly—and now I find myself putting them off. I’ll sit down to do them and end up rearranging papers or thinking about something one of my students said, or, you know, how the district always launches these things like we all have extra hours hidden somewhere. So… that’s why I came in. It just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that broadly. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been really wound up and discouraged.\n\nI still get through the school day, and the kids wouldn’t necessarily know anything is off—I mean, they have enough going on, and honestly some of them are very perceptive—but when I sit down to do lesson plans or answer emails, things I used to just handle, I keep putting them off. So yes, anxious or tense around all of that, but it feels tied to this extra workload more than just all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, kind of discouraged, I guess. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of everything else, I’ve felt really wound up and just… not like myself.\n\nI still get through the school day—because you do, you know, the kids are there and they need you—but then the lesson plans and emails, things I used to handle without thinking, I keep putting off. And then I feel worse about that. So yes, sad in that sense, but it feels very tied to all this extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically liked to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really stopped to measure it that way. I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. It’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about school or work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped anything in a clear way. I still do the usual outside-of-school stuff, as much as I ever did, I think.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F32.0, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I haven’t been feeling like myself lately. The district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to my plate, on top of my regular teaching, and I can still get through the school day, I mean, I’m not falling apart in front of the kids or anything… but I feel so wound up and discouraged.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be pretty on top of lesson plans and emails—maybe too on top of them, honestly—and now I find myself putting them off. I’ll sit down to do them and end up rearranging papers or thinking about something one of my students said, or, you know, how the district always launches these things like we all have extra hours hidden somewhere. So… that’s why I came in. It just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that broadly. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been really wound up and discouraged.\n\nI still get through the school day, and the kids wouldn’t necessarily know anything is off—I mean, they have enough going on, and honestly some of them are very perceptive—but when I sit down to do lesson plans or answer emails, things I used to just handle, I keep putting them off. So yes, anxious or tense around all of that, but it feels tied to this extra workload more than just all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, kind of discouraged, I guess. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of everything else, I’ve felt really wound up and just… not like myself.\n\nI still get through the school day—because you do, you know, the kids are there and they need you—but then the lesson plans and emails, things I used to handle without thinking, I keep putting off. And then I feel worse about that. So yes, sad in that sense, but it feels very tied to all this extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically liked to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really stopped to measure it that way. I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. It’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about school or work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. If I’m truly not thinking about school, I can be okay—like if I’m watching some silly cooking show or talking to my sister about her garden, which is apparently a whole drama this year because the squirrels have become organized.\n\nBut the problem is school sort of sneaks in. Ever since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of everything else, I feel wound up and discouraged more than I usually would. I’m still getting through the day, I’m not falling apart, but I’ll realize I’m putting off lesson plans or emails that I used to just knock out without thinking. So it’s mostly tied to that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change, no. I mean, my sister might say I’m a little harder to get on the phone, but she says that anyway because she thinks everyone should answer immediately, like we’re all sitting next to the landline in 1987.\n\nBut relationships overall… I don’t know. I haven’t had a fight with anyone or pulled away in some dramatic way. I’m still talking to my family and friends. Maybe I’m a little less chatty when school comes up, but that’s about all I can really say.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your worries about work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that closely. Some nights I get into bed and my mind does wander back to school, but then sometimes I’m also thinking about whether I forgot to buy cat food, or that weird noise the refrigerator makes—so I don’t know if I can say it’s specifically work keeping me up.\n\nI mean, I’m sure the school stuff doesn’t help. But trouble sleeping… I’d have to think about that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're putting off tasks because you're worried you might fail at them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of failing. So maybe there’s some worry in there, but it feels more like being overloaded and not quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities outside of school that you've stopped doing or significantly reduced since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped anything in a clear way. I’m still getting through the day, I’m not falling apart, but I’ll realize I’m putting off lesson plans or emails that I used to just knock out without thinking. So it’s mostly tied to that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change, no. I mean, my sister might say I’m a little harder to get on the phone, but she says that anyway because she thinks everyone should answer immediately, like we’re all sitting next to the landline in 1987.\n\nBut relationships overall… I don’t know. I haven’t had a fight with anyone or pulled away in some dramatic way. I’m still talking to my family and friends. Maybe I’m a little less chatty when school comes up, but that’s about all I can really say.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your worries about work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that closely. Some nights I get into bed and my mind does wander back to school, but then sometimes I’m also thinking about whether I forgot to buy cat food, or that weird noise the refrigerator makes—so I don’t know if I can say it’s specifically work keeping me up.\n\nI mean, I’m sure the school stuff doesn’t help. But trouble sleeping… I’d have to think about that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're putting off tasks because you're worried you might fail at them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of failing. It’s more like… ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout onto my regular teaching load, I look at the lesson plans or the emails and I just feel wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nI still get through the day, you know, I’m not collapsing in the copy room or anything—though that copier does test a person’s will to live—but I’ve noticed I’m putting off things I used to just handle without thinking. So maybe there’s some worry in there, but it feels more like being overloaded and not quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities outside of school that you've stopped doing or significantly reduced since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped anything in a clear way. I still do the usual outside-of-school stuff, as much as I ever did, I think.\n\nI mean, I was never exactly climbing mountains after work. My big glamorous evening is more like stopping at the grocery store and then realizing I bought everything except the one thing I went in for. But no, I haven’t noticed some major activity I’ve dropped. Maybe I’m a little slower to make plans sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s because of all this or just… life being life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I haven’t been feeling like myself lately. The district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to my plate, on top of my regular teaching, and I can still get through the school day, I mean, I’m not falling apart in front of the kids or anything… but I feel so wound up and discouraged.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be pretty on top of lesson plans and emails—maybe too on top of them, honestly—and now I find myself putting them off. I’ll sit down to do them and end up rearranging papers or thinking about something one of my students said, or, you know, how the district always launches these things like we all have extra hours hidden somewhere. So… that’s why I came in. It just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that broadly. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been really wound up and discouraged.\n\nI still get through the school day, and the kids wouldn’t necessarily know anything is off—I mean, they have enough going on, and honestly some of them are very perceptive—but when I sit down to do lesson plans or answer emails, things I used to just handle, I keep putting them off. So yes, anxious or tense around all of that, but it feels tied to this extra workload more than just all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, kind of discouraged, I guess. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of everything else, I’ve felt really wound up and just… not like myself.\n\nI still get through the school day—because you do, you know, the kids are there and they need you—but then the lesson plans and emails, things I used to handle without thinking, I keep putting off. And then I feel worse about that. So yes, sad in that sense, but it feels very tied to all this extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically liked to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really stopped to measure it that way. I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. It’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about school or work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. If I’m truly not thinking about school, I can be okay—like if I’m watching some silly cooking show or talking to my sister about her garden, which is apparently a whole drama this year because the squirrels have become organized.\n\nBut the problem is school sort of sneaks in. Ever since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of everything else, I feel wound up and discouraged more than I usually would. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. It’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about school or work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. If I’m truly not thinking about school, I can be okay—like if I’m watching some silly cooking show or talking to my sister about her garden, which is apparently a whole drama this year because the squirrels have become organized.\n\nBut the problem is school sort of sneaks in. Ever since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of everything else, I feel wound up and discouraged more than I usually would. I’m still getting through the day, I’m not falling apart, but I’ll realize I’m putting off lesson plans or emails that I used to just knock out without thinking. So it’s mostly tied to that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change, no. I mean, my sister might say I’m a little harder to get on the phone, but she says that anyway because she thinks everyone should answer immediately, like we’re all sitting next to the landline in 1987.\n\nBut relationships overall… I don’t know. I haven’t had a fight with anyone or pulled away in some dramatic way. I’m still talking to my family and friends. Maybe I’m a little less chatty when school comes up, but that’s about all I can really say.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your worries about work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that closely. Some nights I get into bed and my mind does wander back to school, but then sometimes I’m also thinking about whether I forgot to buy cat food, or that weird noise the refrigerator makes—so I don’t know if I can say it’s specifically work keeping me up.\n\nI mean, I’m sure the school stuff doesn’t help. But trouble sleeping… I’d have to think about that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're putting off tasks because you're worried you might fail at them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of failing. It’s more like… ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout onto my regular teaching load, I look at the lesson plans or the emails and I just feel wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nI still get through the day, you know, I’m not collapsing in the copy room or anything—though that copier does test a person’s will to live—but I’ve noticed I’m putting off things I used to just handle without thinking. So maybe there’s some worry in there, but it feels more like being overloaded and not quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities outside of school that you've stopped doing or significantly reduced since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped anything in a clear way. I still do the usual outside-of-school stuff, as much as I ever did, I think.\n\nI mean, I was never exactly climbing mountains after work. My big glamorous evening is more like stopping at the grocery store and then realizing I bought everything except the one thing I went in for. But no, I haven’t noticed some major activity I’ve dropped. Maybe I’m a little slower to make plans sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s because of all this or just… life being life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling wound up and discouraged since the district added the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: About five months, I think. It started around when the district handed me that rollout role midyear—well, “handed” is generous, it sort of appeared in my inbox like a stray cat you’re suddenly responsible for.\n\nBut yes, roughly five months. Since then I’ve felt more wound up and discouraged than usual.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So yes, anxious or tense around all of that, but it feels tied to this extra workload more than just all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, kind of discouraged, I guess. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of everything else, I’ve felt really wound up and just… not like myself.\n\nI still get through the school day—because you do, you know, the kids are there and they need you—but then the lesson plans and emails, things I used to handle without thinking, I keep putting off. And then I feel worse about that. So yes, sad in that sense, but it feels very tied to all this extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically liked to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really stopped to measure it that way. I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. It’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about school or work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. If I’m truly not thinking about school, I can be okay—like if I’m watching some silly cooking show or talking to my sister about her garden, which is apparently a whole drama this year because the squirrels have become organized.\n\nBut the problem is school sort of sneaks in. Ever since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of everything else, I feel wound up and discouraged more than I usually would. I’m still getting through the day, I’m not falling apart, but I’ll realize I’m putting off lesson plans or emails that I used to just knock out without thinking. So it’s mostly tied to that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change, no. I mean, my sister might say I’m a little harder to get on the phone, but she says that anyway because she thinks everyone should answer immediately, like we’re all sitting next to the landline in 1987.\n\nBut relationships overall… I don’t know. I haven’t had a fight with anyone or pulled away in some dramatic way. I’m still talking to my family and friends. Maybe I’m a little less chatty when school comes up, but that’s about all I can really say.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your worries about work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that closely. Some nights I get into bed and my mind does wander back to school, but then sometimes I’m also thinking about whether I forgot to buy cat food, or that weird noise the refrigerator makes—so I don’t know if I can say it’s specifically work keeping me up.\n\nI mean, I’m sure the school stuff doesn’t help. But trouble sleeping… I’d have to think about that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're putting off tasks because you're worried you might fail at them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of failing. It’s more like… ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout onto my regular teaching load, I look at the lesson plans or the emails and I just feel wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nI still get through the day, you know, I’m not collapsing in the copy room or anything—though that copier does test a person’s will to live—but I’ve noticed I’m putting off things I used to just handle without thinking. So maybe there’s some worry in there, but it feels more like being overloaded and not quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities outside of school that you've stopped doing or significantly reduced since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped anything in a clear way. I still do the usual outside-of-school stuff, as much as I ever did, I think.\n\nI mean, I was never exactly climbing mountains after work. My big glamorous evening is more like stopping at the grocery store and then realizing I bought everything except the one thing I went in for. But no, I haven’t noticed some major activity I’ve dropped. Maybe I’m a little slower to make plans sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s because of all this or just… life being life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling wound up and discouraged since the district added the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: About five months, I think. It started around when the district handed me that rollout role midyear—well, “handed” is generous, it sort of appeared in my inbox like a stray cat you’re suddenly responsible for.\n\nBut yes, roughly five months. Since then I’ve felt more wound up and discouraged than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling wound up and discouraged for more than six months since the district added the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. It’s been about five months, since they assigned me that rollout role midyear. I remember because it was around that point in the school year when everyone’s already counting the weeks until break, and then suddenly there was this whole extra thing to manage. So… about five months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I haven’t been feeling like myself lately. The district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to my plate, on top of my regular teaching, and I can still get through the school day, I mean, I’m not falling apart in front of the kids or anything… but I feel so wound up and discouraged.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be pretty on top of lesson plans and emails—maybe too on top of them, honestly—and now I find myself putting them off. I’ll sit down to do them and end up rearranging papers or thinking about something one of my students said, or, you know, how the district always launches these things like we all have extra hours hidden somewhere. So… that’s why I came in. It just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that broadly. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been really wound up and discouraged.\n\nI still get through the school day, and the kids wouldn’t necessarily know anything is off—I mean, they have enough going on, and honestly some of them are very perceptive—but when I sit down to do lesson plans or answer emails, things I used to just handle, I keep putting them off. So yes, anxious or tense around all of that, but it feels tied to this extra workload more than just all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Sad… yes, kind of discouraged, I guess. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of everything else, I’ve felt really wound up and just… not like myself.\n\nI still get through the school day—because you do, you know, the kids are there and they need you—but then the lesson plans and emails, things I used to handle without thinking, I keep putting off. And then I feel worse about that. So yes, sad in that sense, but it feels very tied to all this extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically liked to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really stopped to measure it that way. I still do some of the things I usually do, but lately my mind drifts back to school stuff so quickly that it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just distracted.\n\nLike I’ll start something at home and then suddenly remember some email I didn’t answer, or I’ll think about one of the SEL materials and whether it even makes sense for my students in both languages, and then I’m not really in the moment anymore. So… I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things, exactly. It’s more like my head won’t stay where I put it.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do but now avoid because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly work things, honestly. I’ve started avoiding lesson plans and emails, which is embarrassing because those used to be automatic for me. I’d sit down, get them done, move on.\n\nNow, since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of regular teaching, I’ll open my laptop and then somehow I’m reorganizing papers or making tea or checking something totally unrelated. And I tell myself, “I’ll do it after dinner,” but then it’s still sitting there.\n\nI still get through the school day, but those tasks outside the actual teaching… I put them off because I feel so wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because you're afraid of not doing them well?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but it doesn’t feel exactly like fear of doing it badly. It’s more that the whole thing feels heavy before I even start.\n\nI came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I still manage to get through the actual school day. I mean, when the students are in front of me, I do what I need to do. But afterward, with lesson plans and emails—things I used to just handle without thinking—I get so wound up and discouraged that I put them off.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something silly, like sorting a stack of worksheets that doesn’t even matter right then. So… yes, I want to do a good job, of course, but the avoiding feels more like I’m overwhelmed by all of it, not just afraid I won’t do it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything significant, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, as far as I can tell. I don’t weigh myself that often, honestly—our scale is kind of tucked away, and I only think of it when I’m cleaning the bathroom or moving things around.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more tired than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say more tired exactly. I mean, teaching is always tiring—by Friday everybody in the building looks like we’ve been through some kind of marathon, and the kids somehow still have full batteries.\n\nBut… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change in my energy in that way. I’m tired some days, sure, but nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is different.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would improve if the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout was removed from your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I think so, probably. It’s hard to know for sure because it’s still on my plate right now, so I haven’t really had the chance to see what I’d be like without it.\n\nI keep imagining if someone just said, “Okay, you don’t have to do that piece anymore,” I’d feel lighter. But then again, schools always find some new initiative—there was a reading one a few years ago with binders and color-coded tabs, and half of us were more confused by the tabs than the actual program.\n\nBut no, this hasn’t gone on after the rollout ended or anything, because it hasn’t ended. It’s still happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about school or work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. If I’m truly not thinking about school, I can be okay—like if I’m watching some silly cooking show or talking to my sister about her garden, which is apparently a whole drama this year because the squirrels have become organized.\n\nBut the problem is school sort of sneaks in. Ever since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of everything else, I feel wound up and discouraged more than I usually would. I’m still getting through the day, I’m not falling apart, but I’ll realize I’m putting off lesson plans or emails that I used to just knock out without thinking. So it’s mostly tied to that, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change, no. I mean, my sister might say I’m a little harder to get on the phone, but she says that anyway because she thinks everyone should answer immediately, like we’re all sitting next to the landline in 1987.\n\nBut relationships overall… I don’t know. I haven’t had a fight with anyone or pulled away in some dramatic way. I’m still talking to my family and friends. Maybe I’m a little less chatty when school comes up, but that’s about all I can really say.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your worries about work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that closely. Some nights I get into bed and my mind does wander back to school, but then sometimes I’m also thinking about whether I forgot to buy cat food, or that weird noise the refrigerator makes—so I don’t know if I can say it’s specifically work keeping me up.\n\nI mean, I’m sure the school stuff doesn’t help. But trouble sleeping… I’d have to think about that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're putting off tasks because you're worried you might fail at them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s exactly fear of failing. It’s more like… ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout onto my regular teaching load, I look at the lesson plans or the emails and I just feel wound up and discouraged before I even start.\n\nI still get through the day, you know, I’m not collapsing in the copy room or anything—though that copier does test a person’s will to live—but I’ve noticed I’m putting off things I used to just handle without thinking. So maybe there’s some worry in there, but it feels more like being overloaded and not quite myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities outside of school that you've stopped doing or significantly reduced since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve stopped anything in a clear way. I still do the usual outside-of-school stuff, as much as I ever did, I think.\n\nI mean, I was never exactly climbing mountains after work. My big glamorous evening is more like stopping at the grocery store and then realizing I bought everything except the one thing I went in for. But no, I haven’t noticed some major activity I’ve dropped. Maybe I’m a little slower to make plans sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s because of all this or just… life being life.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling wound up and discouraged since the district added the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: About five months, I think. It started around when the district handed me that rollout role midyear—well, “handed” is generous, it sort of appeared in my inbox like a stray cat you’re suddenly responsible for.\n\nBut yes, roughly five months. Since then I’ve felt more wound up and discouraged than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling wound up and discouraged for more than six months since the district added the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. It’s been about five months, since they assigned me that rollout role midyear. I remember because it was around that point in the school year when everyone’s already counting the weeks until break, and then suddenly there was this whole extra thing to manage. So… about five months.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt wound up and discouraged every day since the district added the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say every day. That sounds so absolute, and I’m not great at tracking it like that.\n\nSome days are just regular busy teacher days, where you’re thinking about lunch duty and copy paper and why the projector only works when it feels emotionally supported. And then other days it hits me more, especially when that extra rollout stuff is sitting there waiting. But every single day… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping count.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt wound up and discouraged for at least two weeks straight at any point since the district added the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes—though I’m not great at marking it off on a calendar or anything. It’s been going on about five months, since the district assigned me that rollout role midyear.\n\nAnd midyear is such a terrible time to add something, you know? Everyone’s already got their routines, the kids are restless, the copier is somehow always jammed—sorry, that’s beside the point. But yes, during these past five months, there have definitely been stretches where I felt that way for at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad or discouraged for most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day, nearly every day” feels very exact, and I haven’t really been measuring it like that.\n\nThere are days where I feel discouraged, yes, but then I’ll get distracted by the kids or by some ridiculous hallway situation—last week someone lost an entire shoe, somehow—and then I’m just dealing with that. So… I don’t know. I can’t confidently say it’s been most of the day nearly every day for the past two weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not usually, no. I mean, if I’m truly not thinking about school or work, I can be okay.

The problem is school sort of sneaks into everything lately. I came in because since the district added that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt wound up and discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me. I still get through the day—I mean, the kids still need what they need—but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production of it.

So I don’t think it’s anxiety out of nowhere, exactly. It’s more tied to that extra work hanging over me.

The district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout to my plate, on top of my regular teaching, and I can still get through the school day, I mean, I’m not falling apart in front of the kids or anything… but I feel so wound up and discouraged.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be pretty on top of lesson plans and emails—maybe too on top of them, honestly—and now I find myself putting them off. I’ll sit down to do them and end up rearranging papers or thinking about something one of my students said, or, you know, how the district always launches these things like we all have extra hours hidden somewhere.
